quadratics in \(e^x\) or \(2^x\) are plotted as a function with its roots (the solutions) marked on the
\(x\)-axis; simultaneous systems show the two relations meeting; and models show the curve reaching its target.

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 01\quad\small\texttt{(el4-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 01 (el4-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 5^x = 40 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log 5^x = \log 40
\end{gathered}\]
Logarithm is the inverse operation of an exponential, so taking logs is how we get the unknown \( x \) out of the exponent. Whatever you do to one side you must do to the other to keep the equation balanced.

\textit{Step 2: Use the power law to bring \( x \) down.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x \log 5 = \log 40
\end{gathered}\]
The power law \( \log(a^n) = n\log a \) is the whole reason we took logs --- it converts the exponent \( x \) into a coefficient, turning the equation into a linear one in \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Divide both sides by \( \log 5 \) and evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\log 40}{\log 5} \approx \frac{1.6021}{0.6990} \approx 2.29
\end{gathered}\]
Now \( x \) is a simple multiple of \( \log 5 \), so divide to isolate it. Sanity check: \( 5^2 = 25 \) and \( 5^3 = 125 \), so \( x \approx 2.29 \) sits sensibly between.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 2.29 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 02\quad\small\texttt{(el4-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 02 (el4-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 3^x = 70 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(3^x) = \log 70
\end{gathered}\]
Logs are the inverse of exponentials, so applying \( \log \) is what lets us extract the unknown from the exponent. The equation stays balanced because the same operation is done to both sides.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log 3 = \log 70
\end{gathered}\]
The power law \( \log(a^n) = n\log a \) drops the exponent down to become a coefficient. The equation is now linear in \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\log 70}{\log 3} \approx \frac{1.8451}{0.4771} \approx 3.87
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by \( \log 3 \) to isolate \( x \). Sanity check: \( 3^3 = 27 \) and \( 3^4 = 81 \), so \( x \approx 3.87 \) is in the right ballpark.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 3.87 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 03\quad\small\texttt{(el4-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 03 (el4-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 0.6^x = 0.1 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(0.6^x) = \log(0.1)
\end{gathered}\]
Logs reverse exponentials, which is what we need to free the unknown \( x \) from the exponent. The method works just as well when the base is less than 1.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log(0.6) = \log(0.1)
\end{gathered}\]
The power law brings \( x \) down to a coefficient. Note that \( \log 0.1 = -1 \) since \( 10^{-1} = 0.1 \), and \( \log 0.6 \) is also negative because \( 0.6 < 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\log(0.1)}{\log(0.6)} = \frac{-1}{-0.2218} \approx 4.51
\end{gathered}\]
Both logs are negative, and a negative divided by a negative is positive --- that matches our expectation, since \( 0.6 \) raised to a positive power gets smaller. Always sign-check at the end.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 4.51 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 04\quad\small\texttt{(el4-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 04 (el4-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 2^{3x} = 100 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(2^{3x}) = \log 100
\end{gathered}\]
Applying \( \log \) is the inverse of an exponential --- it lets us pull the whole exponent \( 3x \) down where we can manipulate it algebraically.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x\log 2 = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The power law brings the entire exponent \( 3x \) down as a factor. The right side simplifies neatly because \( \log_{10} 100 = 2 \) exactly.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{2}{3\log 2} = \frac{2}{3 \times 0.3010} \approx \frac{2}{0.9031} \approx 2.21
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by \( 3\log 2 \) to isolate \( x \). Check: \( 2^{3 \times 2.21} = 2^{6.63} \approx 99 \), close to 100, so the answer is reasonable.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 2.21 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 05\quad\small\texttt{(el4-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 05 (el4-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 4^{x-1} = 30 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(4^{x-1}) = \log 30
\end{gathered}\]
Taking logs is the inverse of an exponential --- it lets us pull the exponent down so \( x \) is no longer locked above the base.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x-1)\log 4 = \log 30
\end{gathered}\]
The power law \( \log(a^n) = n\log a \) brings the whole exponent down --- including the \( -1 \). Keep the bracket around \( (x-1) \) so the subtraction isn't lost.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x - 1 \), then for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x - 1 = \frac{\log 30}{\log 4} \approx \frac{1.4771}{0.6021} \approx 2.453 \Rightarrow x \approx 3.45
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by \( \log 4 \) to get \( x-1 \), then add 1. Check: \( 4^{2.45} \approx 30 \) $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 3.45 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 06\quad\small\texttt{(el4-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 06 (el4-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 6^{2x+1} = 200 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log_{10} \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(6^{2x+1}) = \log 200
\end{gathered}\]
Taking logs is the inverse of an exponential, which is what we need in order to extract the unknown \( x \) from the exponent.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2x+1)\log 6 = \log 200
\end{gathered}\]
The power law brings the entire exponent \( (2x+1) \) down as a factor. Keep it bracketed so neither term is dropped.

\textit{Step 3: Divide by \( \log 6 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 1 = \frac{\log 200}{\log 6} \approx \frac{2.3010}{0.7782} \approx 2.957
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate the linear expression in \( x \) by dividing both sides by \( \log 6 \), then evaluate the fraction.

\textit{Step 4: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x = 1.957 \Rightarrow x \approx 0.979
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract 1, then divide by 2. Check: \( 6^{2(0.979)+1} = 6^{2.958} \approx 200 \) $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 0.979 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 07\quad\small\texttt{(el4-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 07 (el4-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 5^x = 2^{x+3} \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log(5^x) = \log(2^{x+3})
\end{gathered}\]
When \( x \) appears in two different exponents, taking logs is the inverse step that brings both down to the same level so we can solve a linear equation.

\textit{Step 2: Apply the power law to each side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log 5 = (x+3)\log 2
\end{gathered}\]
The power law converts each exponent into a coefficient. Bracket \( (x+3) \) on the right so the \( +3 \) isn't lost when expanding.

\textit{Step 3: Expand and collect \( x \) terms on one side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log 5 = x\log 2 + 3\log 2 \Rightarrow x\log 5 - x\log 2 = 3\log 2
\end{gathered}\]
Treat \( \log 5 \) and \( \log 2 \) as constants. Move all \( x \)-terms to one side so we can factorise.

\textit{Step 4: Factorise and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(\log 5 - \log 2) = 3\log 2 \Rightarrow x = \frac{3\log 2}{\log 5 - \log 2} = \frac{3\log 2}{\log 2.5} \approx \frac{0.9031}{0.3979} \approx 2.27
\end{gathered}\]
Factor out \( x \) then divide. Using the quotient law \( \log 5 - \log 2 = \log(5/2) = \log 2.5 \) makes the arithmetic cleaner because we evaluate one log instead of two.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 2.27 \)
\end{answerbox}

\node[red!70!black,scale=0.72,anchor=center,inner sep=1pt] at (7.955,3.625) {$2^{x+3}$};

\node[above left,scale=0.72] at (4.88,2.799) {$x\approx2.27$};

}
\end{center}
\small The equation shown graphically, with the solution(s) marked.\normalsize

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 08\quad\small\texttt{(el4-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 08 (el4-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 3^{x+2} = 7^x \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take \( \log \) of both sides and apply the power law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+2)\log 3 = x\log 7
\end{gathered}\]
With \( x \) appearing inside two different exponents, taking logs is the inverse step needed to bring both \( x \)-exponents down to coefficient level.

\textit{Step 2: Expand the left side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\log 3 + 2\log 3 = x\log 7
\end{gathered}\]
Distribute \( \log 3 \) over \( (x+2) \). Now both sides are linear in \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Collect \( x \) terms.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2\log 3 = x\log 7 - x\log 3 = x(\log 7 - \log 3)
\end{gathered}\]
Move \( x\log 3 \) to the right so the \( x \)-terms are on one side and the constant on the other, then factor out \( x \).

\textit{Step 4: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{2\log 3}{\log 7 - \log 3} = \frac{2\log 3}{\log(7/3)} \approx \frac{0.9542}{0.3679} \approx 2.59
\end{gathered}\]
Divide to isolate \( x \). Combining \( \log 7 - \log 3 = \log(7/3) \) via the quotient law is optional but makes the denominator a single log for cleaner calculation.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 2.59 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 09\quad\small\texttt{(el4-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 09 (el4-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the value of \( x \) given that \( \log_3(4x) = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write in index form using \( \log_a b = c \Leftrightarrow a^c = b \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3^4 = 4x
\end{gathered}\]
Rewriting in index form is the inverse move of taking a log --- it undoes the logarithm so we can work with \( 4x \) directly. The base of the log becomes the base of the power, and the right-hand side becomes the exponent.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate \( 3^4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
81 = 4x
\end{gathered}\]
\( 3^4 = 81 \). Evaluating the constant first turns the equation into a simple linear one.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{81}{4} = 20.25
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 4. Check: \( \log_3(4 \times 20.25) = \log_3 81 = 4 \) $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 20.25 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 10\quad\small\texttt{(el4-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 10 (el4-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the value of \( x \) given that \( \log_5(x + 2) = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Convert to index form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
5^3 = x + 2
\end{gathered}\]
Index form is the inverse of log form: \( \log_a b = c \Leftrightarrow a^c = b \). This removes the logarithm so we can solve for \( x \) directly.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
125 = x + 2 \Rightarrow x = 123
\end{gathered}\]
\( 5^3 = 125 \); subtract 2 to isolate \( x \). Validity check: \( x+2 = 125 > 0 \), so the log argument is positive $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 123 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 11\quad\small\texttt{(el4-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 11 (el4-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the value of \( x \) given that \( \log_4(3 - x) = 2.5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write in index form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4^{2.5} = 3 - x
\end{gathered}\]
Converting to index form removes the logarithm --- it is the inverse of taking a log. The base of the log becomes the base of the power.

\textit{Step 2: Evaluate \( 4^{2.5} = 4^{5/2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4^{5/2} = (\sqrt{4})^5 = 2^5 = 32
\end{gathered}\]
A fractional power \( \tfrac{5}{2} \) means take the square root (denominator) and raise to the 5th (numerator). Doing the root first keeps the numbers small.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
32 = 3 - x \Rightarrow x = 3 - 32 = -29
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange to isolate \( x \). Validity check: \( 3 - (-29) = 32 > 0 \), so the log argument is positive $\checkmark$. Don't reject \( x = -29 \) just for being negative --- only the log's argument needs to be positive.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = -29 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 12\quad\small\texttt{(el4-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 12 (el4-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 5\log_{10}(x) = 3 \), giving your answer to 3 significant figures.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Divide both sides by 5.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_{10}(x) = \frac{3}{5} = 0.6
\end{gathered}\]
Always isolate the log term before exponentiating --- otherwise the coefficient gets in the way of the cancellation.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( 10^{\square} \) to both sides (the inverse of \( \log_{10} \)).}
\[\begin{gathered}
10^{\log_{10}(x)} = 10^{0.6}
\end{gathered}\]
Raising 10 to the power of each side undoes the \( \log_{10} \), because exponential and log with the same base are inverse functions.

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 10^{0.6} \approx 3.98
\end{gathered}\]
The left side simplifies to \( x \) by cancellation. Check: \( 5\log_{10}(3.98) \approx 5 \times 0.5999 \approx 3 \) $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x \approx 3.98 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 13\quad\small\texttt{(el4-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 13 (el4-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 4e^x = 60 \), giving (i) an exact answer and (ii) an answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Divide both sides by 4.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 15
\end{gathered}\]
Always isolate the exponential before applying \( \ln \) --- otherwise the constant blocks the clean cancellation.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 15
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^{\square} \), so \( \ln(e^x) = x \) by direct cancellation. This is how we free \( x \) from the exponent.

\textit{Step 3: Evaluate to 3 s.f.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 15 \approx 2.71
\end{gathered}\]
Use a calculator for the decimal. Check: \( 4e^{2.71} \approx 4 \times 15 = 60 \) $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (i) \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 15 \) \quad  (ii) \(\displaystyle  x \approx 2.71 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 14\quad\small\texttt{(el4-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 14 (el4-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( e^{0.5x} + 3 = 10 \), giving your answer as an exact value.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Subtract 3 from both sides to isolate the exponential.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{0.5x} = 7
\end{gathered}\]
Always isolate the exponential term before applying \( \ln \). If you took logs while the \( +3 \) was still there, \( \ln \) of a sum would not simplify.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.5x = \ln 7
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^{\square} \), so \( \ln(e^{0.5x}) = 0.5x \) by cancellation. The whole exponent comes through unchanged.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2\ln 7
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by 2 to undo the \( 0.5 \) coefficient. Leaving the answer as \( 2\ln 7 \) keeps it exact.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 2\ln 7 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 15\quad\small\texttt{(el4-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 15 (el4-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 8 - 2e^{x-1} = 0 \), giving your answer as an exact value.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange to isolate the exponential.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2e^{x-1} = 8 \Rightarrow e^{x-1} = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Move the exponential to one side and divide by 2 to leave it bare. The exponential must be alone before we apply \( \ln \).

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x - 1 = \ln 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^{\square} \), so \( \ln(e^{x-1}) = x-1 \). The whole exponent comes down as is.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 + \ln 4
\end{gathered}\]
Add 1 to both sides. Could also be written as \( 1 + 2\ln 2 \) using the power law, but \( 1 + \ln 4 \) is simpler.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 1 + \ln 4 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 16\quad\small\texttt{(el4-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 16 (el4-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \ln(3x) = 5 \), giving your answer as an exact value.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply \( e^{\square} \) to both sides to remove the \( \ln \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{\ln(3x)} = e^5
\end{gathered}\]
Exponentiating with base \( e \) is the inverse of \( \ln \), so it cancels the logarithm and frees the argument \( 3x \).

\textit{Step 2: Simplify and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x = e^5 \Rightarrow x = \frac{e^5}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{\ln(3x)} = 3x \) by inverse cancellation, then divide by 3. Validity check: \( 3x = e^5 > 0 \), so the log argument is valid $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{e^5}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 17\quad\small\texttt{(el4-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 17 (el4-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 4\ln(2x - 1) = 12 \), giving your answer as an exact value.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Divide both sides by 4.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(2x-1) = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate the logarithm first --- the coefficient 4 must be removed before exponentiating so the inverse cancellation works cleanly.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( e^{\square} \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 1 = e^3
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{\square} \) is the inverse of \( \ln \), so \( e^{\ln(2x-1)} = 2x-1 \). This frees the argument from the log.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x = e^3 + 1 \Rightarrow x = \frac{e^3 + 1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Add 1, then divide by 2. Validity check: \( e^3 + 1 > 0 \) so \( 2x - 1 > 0 \), meaning the log argument is positive $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{e^3 + 1}{2} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 18\quad\small\texttt{(el4-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 18 (el4-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( e^{3x} = 27 \), giving your answer in terms of \( \ln 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x = \ln 27
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^{\square} \), so it brings the exponent \( 3x \) down directly. This is exactly how we isolate the unknown.

\textit{Step 2: Write \( \ln 27 \) in terms of \( \ln 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln 27 = \ln(3^3) = 3\ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Spotting that \( 27 = 3^3 \) lets the power law convert \( \ln 27 \) into a multiple of \( \ln 3 \), which matches the form the question asks for.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x = 3\ln 3 \Rightarrow x = \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 3. The factor of 3 cancels neatly, giving a clean exact answer.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 3 \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]
\begin{scope}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 19\quad\small\texttt{(el4-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 19 (el4-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( e^{2x - 1} = 9 \), giving your answer in terms of \( \ln 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 1 = \ln 9
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) and \( e^{\square} \) are inverse operations, so \( \ln(e^{2x-1}) = 2x - 1 \) by cancellation --- the whole exponent comes down.

\textit{Step 2: Write \( \ln 9 \) in terms of \( \ln 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln 9 = \ln(3^2) = 2\ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( 9 = 3^2 \), the power law converts \( \ln 9 \) into \( 2\ln 3 \), matching the form requested by the question.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 1 = 2\ln 3 \Rightarrow x = \frac{1 + 2\ln 3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Add 1 to both sides, then divide by 2. Leaving the answer as a single fraction keeps it tidy and exact.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{1 + 2\ln 3}{2} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 20\quad\small\texttt{(el4-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 20 (el4-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \ln 5 + \ln x = 7 \), giving your answer as an exact value.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Use the product law to combine the left side.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(5x) = 7
\end{gathered}\]
The product law \( \ln a + \ln b = \ln(ab) \) merges two log terms into one, so we have a single logarithm to invert.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( e^{\square} \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
5x = e^7
\end{gathered}\]
Exponentiating with base \( e \) is the inverse of \( \ln \), so \( e^{\ln(5x)} = 5x \) by cancellation.

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{e^7}{5}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 5. Validity check: \( 5x = e^7 > 0 \) so \( x > 0 \), making the original \( \ln x \) valid $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{e^7}{5} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 21\quad\small\texttt{(el4-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 21 (el4-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \ln(4x) + \ln(3x) = 10 \), giving your answer as an exact value.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine the left side using the product law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln(12x^2) = 10
\end{gathered}\]
The product law \( \ln a + \ln b = \ln(ab) \) lets us merge into one log: \( 4x \cdot 3x = 12x^2 \). Now a single \( e^{\square} \) will free the argument.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( e^{\square} \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x^2 = e^{10}
\end{gathered}\]
Exponentiating is the inverse of \( \ln \), so the log on the left cancels to leave its argument \( 12x^2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve for \( x \), taking the positive root only.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = \frac{e^{10}}{12} \Rightarrow x = \sqrt{\frac{e^{10}}{12}} = \frac{e^5}{\sqrt{12}} = \frac{e^5}{2\sqrt{3}}
\end{gathered}\]
Although squaring loses sign information, the original \( \ln(4x) \) and \( \ln(3x) \) require \( x > 0 \), so we reject the negative root. Note \( \sqrt{e^{10}} = e^5 \) and \( \sqrt{12} = 2\sqrt{3} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{e^5}{2\sqrt{3}} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 22\quad\small\texttt{(el4-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 22 (el4-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \ln(x - 3) - \ln(2x) = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln\!\left(\frac{x-3}{2x}\right) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The quotient law \( \ln a - \ln b = \ln(a/b) \) merges two log terms into one, ready to invert in a single step.

\textit{Step 2: Apply \( e^{\square} \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{x-3}{2x} = e^0 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Exponentiating undoes \( \ln \). The right side becomes \( e^0 = 1 \), giving a simple rational equation.

\textit{Step 3: Solve the resulting equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x - 3 = 2x \Rightarrow -3 = x
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply both sides by \( 2x \) to clear the fraction, then rearrange. This gives a candidate value for \( x \) which we must check.

\textit{Step 4: Check validity: \( x = -3 \) gives \( \ln(-3-3) \) --- argument is negative. Reject.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{No valid solution}
\end{gathered}\]
Both \( x-3 \) and \( 2x \) must be positive for the original logs to be defined. \( x = -3 \) gives \( x - 3 = -6 \) and \( 2x = -6 \), both negative --- so this candidate is rejected and the equation has no solution.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No solution (the value \(\displaystyle  x = -3 \) is invalid as the log arguments would be negative).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 23\quad\small\texttt{(el4-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 23 (el4-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 3\ln x + 5\ln x = 2 \), giving your answer as an exact value.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Collect the \( \ln x \) terms on the left.}
\[\begin{gathered}
8\ln x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Treat \( \ln x \) as a single quantity and add the coefficients: \( 3 + 5 = 8 \). This is just like collecting \( 3y + 5y = 8y \).

\textit{Step 2: Divide both sides by 8.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln x = \frac{1}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
Always isolate the logarithm before exponentiating --- the coefficient blocks the cancellation otherwise.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( e^{\square} \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = e^{1/4}
\end{gathered}\]
\( e^{\square} \) is the inverse of \( \ln \), so \( e^{\ln x} = x \) by cancellation. The result is positive, satisfying the domain of \( \ln x \) $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = e^{1/4} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 24\quad\small\texttt{(el4-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 24 (el4-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the exact solutions of \( e^{2x} - 5e^x + 6 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Note that \( e^{2x} = (e^x)^2 \) and substitute \( y = e^x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y^2 - 5y + 6 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The substitution \( y = e^x \) hides the exponential and reveals a familiar quadratic. We use this because \( e^{2x} = (e^x)^2 = y^2 \), so the equation becomes algebraic in \( y \).

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(y-2)(y-3) = 0 \Rightarrow y = 2 \text{ or } y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
We need two numbers that multiply to \( +6 \) and add to \( -5 \): namely \( -2 \) and \( -3 \), giving the factors \( (y-2)(y-3) \).

\textit{Step 3: Replace \( y \) with \( e^x \) and apply \( \ln \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 2 \Rightarrow x = \ln 2 \qquad e^x = 3 \Rightarrow x = \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Both \( y = 2 \) and \( y = 3 \) are positive, so both are valid (since \( e^x > 0 \) for all real \( x \), we must always reject any non-positive \( y \)). Taking \( \ln \) of both equations gives the two solutions.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 2 \) or \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 25\quad\small\texttt{(el4-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 25 (el4-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the exact solutions of \( e^{2x} - 7e^x + 12 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( y = e^x \) so \( e^{2x} = y^2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y^2 - 7y + 12 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The substitution \( y = e^x \) converts the exponential equation into a standard quadratic. Always remember the implicit restriction \( y > 0 \), since \( e^x \) is always positive.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(y-3)(y-4) = 0 \Rightarrow y = 3 \text{ or } y = 4
\end{gathered}\]
We need two numbers that multiply to \( +12 \) and add to \( -7 \): namely \( -3 \) and \( -4 \).

\textit{Step 3: Undo the substitution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 3 \Rightarrow x = \ln 3 \qquad e^x = 4 \Rightarrow x = \ln 4
\end{gathered}\]
Both roots are positive, so both are valid. Apply \( \ln \) (the inverse of \( e^{\square} \)) to each to find \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 3 \) or \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 4 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 26\quad\small\texttt{(el4-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 26 (el4-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the exact solutions of \( 2e^{2x} - 7e^x + 3 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( y = e^x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2y^2 - 7y + 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( e^{2x} = (e^x)^2 \), the substitution \( y = e^x \) turns the exponential equation into a quadratic in \( y \). Remember \( y > 0 \) --- any non-positive root must be rejected.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(2y - 1)(y - 3) = 0 \Rightarrow y = \frac{1}{2} \text{ or } y = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Check by expanding: \( (2y-1)(y-3) = 2y^2 - 6y - y + 3 = 2y^2 - 7y + 3 \) $\checkmark$.

\textit{Step 3: Undo the substitution. Both values of \( y \) are positive, so both are valid.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = \frac{1}{2} \Rightarrow x = \ln\frac{1}{2} = -\ln 2 \qquad e^x = 3 \Rightarrow x = \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Both roots are positive so both survive the \( y > 0 \) check. Using the power law, \( \ln\tfrac{1}{2} = \ln(2^{-1}) = -\ln 2 \), giving a cleaner exact form.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = -\ln 2 \) or \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 27\quad\small\texttt{(el4-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 27 (el4-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the exact solutions of \( 3e^{2x} + 10e^x + 3 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let \( y = e^x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3y^2 + 10y + 3 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The substitution \( y = e^x \) (with \( y > 0 \)) turns the exponential equation into a quadratic. We will need to check that any roots satisfy \( y > 0 \) before undoing the substitution.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(3y + 1)(y + 3) = 0 \Rightarrow y = -\frac{1}{3} \text{ or } y = -3
\end{gathered}\]
Expand to check: \( (3y+1)(y+3) = 3y^2 + 9y + y + 3 = 3y^2 + 10y + 3 \) $\checkmark$.

\textit{Step 3: Check validity of each solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = -\frac{1}{3} \text{ - impossible} \qquad e^x = -3 \text{ - impossible}
\end{gathered}\]
Both roots are negative, but \( e^x > 0 \) for every real \( x \), so both must be rejected. This is exactly why the \( y > 0 \) restriction matters --- the original equation has no real solutions.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No real solutions.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 28\quad\small\texttt{(el4-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 28 (el4-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve simultaneously: \( 3^x = 9^y \) and \( 2x + y = 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Write \( 9^y = (3^2)^y = 3^{2y} \). Since \( 3^x = 3^{2y} \), the exponents must be equal.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2y
\end{gathered}\]
Rewriting 9 as \( 3^2 \) gives the same base on both sides. When two equal exponentials share a base, their exponents must match --- no need to take logs.

\textit{Step 2: Substitute \( x = 2y \) into the second equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2(2y) + y = 5 \Rightarrow 5y = 5 \Rightarrow y = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Substitution converts the system into a single linear equation in \( y \), which solves cleanly.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2y = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Back-substitute \( y = 1 \) into \( x = 2y \). Check: \( 3^2 = 9 = 9^1 \) $\checkmark$ and \( 2(2) + 1 = 5 \) $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 2, \; y = 1 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 29\quad\small\texttt{(el4-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 29 (el4-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve simultaneously: \( \log_2 x = 1 + \log_2 y \) and \( x = 3y - 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange the first equation using the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_2 x - \log_2 y = 1 \Rightarrow \log_2\!\left(\frac{x}{y}\right) = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Move \( \log_2 y \) to the left, then apply \( \log a - \log b = \log(a/b) \) to combine into a single logarithm --- easier to invert in one step.

\textit{Step 2: Convert to index form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{x}{y} = 2^1 = 2 \Rightarrow x = 2y
\end{gathered}\]
Index form is the inverse of log form, so it removes the logarithm and gives a simple algebraic relationship between \( x \) and \( y \).

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = 2y \) into the second equation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2y = 3y - 4 \Rightarrow y = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Now we have a single linear equation in \( y \), which gives \( y = 4 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2(4) = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Back-substitute \( y = 4 \). Both \( x = 8 > 0 \) and \( y = 4 > 0 \), so the log arguments are valid $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 8, \; y = 4 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 30\quad\small\texttt{(el4-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 30 (el4-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A population of rabbits is modelled by \( P = 120e^{0.04t} \), where \( P \) is the population and \( t \) is the time in years. \\[3pt]  (a) Find the initial population. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the value of \( t \) when the population reaches 300. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P = 120e^{0.04 \times 0} = 120e^0 = 120
\end{gathered}\]
At the initial time \( t = 0 \) the exponential factor is \( e^0 = 1 \), so \( P \) equals the coefficient in front. The initial population is 120 rabbits.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( P = 300 \) and solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
120e^{0.04t} = 300 \Rightarrow e^{0.04t} = \frac{300}{120} = 2.5
\end{gathered}\]
Isolate the exponential by dividing through by 120 --- the coefficient must be removed before applying \( \ln \).

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
0.04t = \ln(2.5)
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^{\square} \), so it brings the exponent \( 0.04t \) down. This is exactly the step that releases \( t \) from the exponent.

\textit{Step 4: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln(2.5)}{0.04} \approx \frac{0.9163}{0.04} \approx 22.9
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by the coefficient \( 0.04 \) to isolate \( t \). Sanity check: a slow growth rate of 4\% per year takes a long time to roughly triple the population, so \textasciitilde 23 years is reasonable.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  P = 120 \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  t \approx 22.9 \) years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 31\quad\small\texttt{(el4-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 31 (el4-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The temperature \( T \) (\textdegree C) of a cooling drink is modelled by \( T = 80e^{-0.05t} + 20 \), where \( t \) is the time in minutes. \\[3pt]  (a) State the initial temperature. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the time at which the drink reaches 40\textdegree C. Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
T = 80e^0 + 20 = 80 + 20 = 100
\end{gathered}\]
At \( t = 0 \) the exponential is \( e^0 = 1 \), so the temperature equals \( 80 + 20 = 100^\circ\!\text{C} \). The \( +20 \) acts as the ambient (room) temperature the drink decays towards.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( T = 40 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
80e^{-0.05t} + 20 = 40 \Rightarrow 80e^{-0.05t} = 20 \Rightarrow e^{-0.05t} = 0.25
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract 20 then divide by 80 to isolate the exponential. Both constants must be removed before applying \( \ln \) so the inverse cancellation works cleanly.

\textit{Step 3: Apply \( \ln \) to both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-0.05t = \ln(0.25) = \ln\!\left(\frac{1}{4}\right) = -\ln 4
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^{\square} \), so the exponent comes down. Rewriting \( \ln(0.25) = \ln(4^{-1}) = -\ln 4 \) via the power law gives a tidier exact form.

\textit{Step 4: Solve for \( t \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = \frac{\ln 4}{0.05} \approx \frac{1.3863}{0.05} \approx 27.7
\end{gathered}\]
The two negatives on the left cancel when dividing, leaving a positive value for \( t \) --- physically sensible since cooling takes a positive amount of time.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  100\,^\circ\!\text{C} \) \quad  (b) \(\displaystyle  t \approx 27.7 \) minutes
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 32\quad\small\texttt{(el4-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 32 (el4-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{(a) Show that the equation \( e^{2x} - 4e^x = 5 \) can be written as \( (e^x - 5)(e^x + 1) = 0 \). \\[3pt]  (b) Hence solve \( e^{2x} - 4e^x = 5 \), giving any exact solutions.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Rearrange: subtract 5 from both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{2x} - 4e^x - 5 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move all terms to one side so we have the standard form \( = 0 \). This sets up the quadratic-in-\( e^x \) structure.

\textit{Step 2: Let \( y = e^x \) and factorise \( y^2 - 4y - 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
(y-5)(y+1) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Two numbers multiplying to \( -5 \) and adding to \( -4 \) are \( -5 \) and \( +1 \). Replacing \( y \) by \( e^x \) gives \( (e^x - 5)(e^x + 1) = 0 \) as required $\checkmark$.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Solve each factor.}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 5 \Rightarrow x = \ln 5 \qquad e^x = -1 \Rightarrow \text{no real solution}
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( e^x > 0 \) for every real \( x \), the root \( e^x = -1 \) must be rejected. Only the positive root \( e^x = 5 \) gives a valid solution \( x = \ln 5 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \ln 5 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 33\quad\small\texttt{(el4-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 33 (el4-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{(a) Show that \( \log_3(x+6) - \log_3 x = 2 \) leads to \( 9x = x + 6 \). \\[3pt]  (b) Hence solve \( \log_3(x+6) - \log_3 x = 2 \), giving your answer as an exact value.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Apply the quotient law.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3\!\left(\frac{x+6}{x}\right) = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The quotient law \( \log a - \log b = \log(a/b) \) merges the two log terms into a single logarithm, which we can then invert in one step.

\textit{Step 2: Convert to index form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{x+6}{x} = 3^2 = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Index form is the inverse of log form, so this removes the logarithm. \( \log_a b = c \Leftrightarrow a^c = b \).

\textit{Step 3: Multiply both sides by \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 6 = 9x
\end{gathered}\]
Clear the fraction to get the required form $\checkmark$.

\textit{Step 4: (b) Solve for \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6 = 8x \Rightarrow x = \frac{6}{8} = \frac{3}{4}
\end{gathered}\]
Subtract \( x \) from both sides, then divide by 8 to isolate \( x \).

\textit{Step 5: Check validity: \( x = \frac{3}{4} > 0 \) \textbackslash checkmark}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3\!\left(\frac{3}{4} + 6\right) - \log_3\!\left(\frac{3}{4}\right) = \log_3\!\left(\frac{27}{4} \times \frac{4}{3}\right) = \log_3 9 = 2 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
Both log arguments \( x \) and \( x+6 \) are positive at \( x = \tfrac{3}{4} \), and substituting back recovers the original equation --- so the solution is valid.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{3}{4} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 34\quad\small\texttt{(el4-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 34 (el4-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The value \( V \) (\pounds ) of a car after \( t \) years is modelled by \( V = 18000 \times 0.85^t \). \\[3pt]  (a) Write down the initial value of the car. \\[3pt]  (b) Find the number of complete years for the car to first be worth less than \pounds 6000.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Substitute \( t = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = 18000 \times 0.85^0 = 18000
\end{gathered}\]
At \( t = 0 \) the decay factor is \( 0.85^0 = 1 \), so the initial value equals the coefficient: \pounds 18 000.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Set \( V < 6000 \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
18000 \times 0.85^t < 6000 \Rightarrow 0.85^t < \frac{1}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide both sides by 18 000 (a positive number, so the inequality direction is preserved) to isolate the exponential.

\textit{Step 3: Take \( \log \) of both sides.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t\log(0.85) < \log\!\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)
\end{gathered}\]
Logs are the inverse of exponentials and \( \log \) is monotonically increasing on \( (0, \infty) \), so the inequality direction is preserved when we take logs of two positive sides. The power law then brings the exponent \( t \) down.

\textit{Step 4: Divide by \( \log(0.85) \). Note: \( \log(0.85) < 0 \) so the inequality reverses.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t > \frac{\log(1/3)}{\log(0.85)} = \frac{-0.4771}{-0.0706} \approx 6.76
\end{gathered}\]
Crucial pitfall: \( 0.85 < 1 \) means \( \log(0.85) \) is negative, so dividing by it flips the inequality from \( < \) to \( > \). Always check the sign of what you divide an inequality by.

\textit{Step 5: Interpret the result.}
\[\begin{gathered}
t = 7 \text{ complete years}
\end{gathered}\]
We need the smallest whole number of years with \( t > 6.76 \); the first complete year that satisfies this is \( t = 7 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \pounds 18 000 \quad  (b) After \(\displaystyle  7 \) complete years
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 35\quad\small\texttt{(el4-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations Involving Logarithms 35 (el4-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{(a) Solve \( \ln(x^2 - 2x) = \ln(8 - x^2) \). \\[3pt]  (b) Verify that both solutions are valid by checking that the logarithm arguments are positive.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Since both sides are \( \ln \) of the same base, equate the arguments.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 2x = 8 - x^2
\end{gathered}\]
The \( \ln \) function is one-to-one (strictly increasing), so if \( \ln A = \ln B \) then \( A = B \). This is what removes the logs without needing to exponentiate.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange into standard quadratic form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2 - 2x - 8 = 0 \Rightarrow x^2 - x - 4 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move every term to one side, then divide by the common factor 2 to keep coefficients small.

\textit{Step 3: Solve using the quadratic formula.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 16}}{2} = \frac{1 \pm \sqrt{17}}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
With \( a=1, b=-1, c=-4 \) the discriminant is \( b^2 - 4ac = 1 + 16 = 17 \), giving two real candidates. Each must still be checked against the log domain.

\textit{Step 4: (b) Check \( x = \frac{1 + \sqrt{17}}{2} \approx 2.56 \): argument \( x^2 - 2x \approx 6.55 - 5.12 \approx 1.43 > 0 \) \textbackslash checkmark}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{1+\sqrt{17}}{2} \text{ is valid}
\end{gathered}\]
Both \( x^2 - 2x \) and \( 8 - x^2 \) must be positive for the original \( \ln \) terms. For \( x \approx 2.56 \) we have \( 8 - x^2 \approx 8 - 6.55 = 1.45 > 0 \) too, so this root is valid.

\textit{Step 5: Check \( x = \frac{1 - \sqrt{17}}{2} \approx -1.56 \): argument \( x^2 - 2x \approx 2.43 + 3.12 \approx 5.55 > 0 \) \textbackslash checkmark}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{1-\sqrt{17}}{2} \text{ is also valid}
\end{gathered}\]
For \( x \approx -1.56 \), \( 8 - x^2 \approx 8 - 2.43 \approx 5.57 > 0 \) as well. So both candidates satisfy the domain restrictions and both are accepted.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{1 + \sqrt{17}}{2} \) or \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{1 - \sqrt{17}}{2} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 36\quad\small\texttt{(el4-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 36 (el4-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 1}

\question{Solve \( 2^x = 64 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Match bases}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^x = 2^6 \implies x = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Recognising \( 64 = 2^6 \) lets us avoid logs entirely: with equal bases, the exponents must be equal.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 6\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 37\quad\small\texttt{(el4-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 37 (el4-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 3^x = 7 \). Give your answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\ln 7}{\ln 3} = \frac{1.946}{1.099} = 1.77
\end{gathered}\]
Taking \( \ln \) of both sides of \( 3^x = 7 \) gives \( x\ln 3 = \ln 7 \); divide by \( \ln 3 \) to isolate \( x \). Sanity check: \( 3^1 = 3 \) and \( 3^2 = 9 \), so \( x \approx 1.77 \) fits.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 1.77\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 38\quad\small\texttt{(el4-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 38 (el4-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( 5^{x-1} = 25 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Match bases}
\[\begin{gathered}
5^{x-1} = 5^2 \implies x - 1 = 2 \implies x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( 25 = 5^2 \), both sides share the base 5, so we can equate the exponents directly without needing logs.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 39\quad\small\texttt{(el4-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 39 (el4-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( e^{3x} = 20 \). Give exact answer.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x = \ln 20 \implies x = \frac{\ln 20}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^{\square} \), so taking \( \ln \) brings the exponent \( 3x \) down; divide by 3 to isolate \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = (ln 20)/3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 40\quad\small\texttt{(el4-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 40 (el4-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 4e^x - 12 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Rearrange}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^x = 3 \implies x = \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Add 12 then divide by 4 to isolate the exponential, then apply \( \ln \) (the inverse of \( e^{\square} \)) to bring the exponent down.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = ln 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 41\quad\small\texttt{(el4-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 41 (el4-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \ln(2x - 1) = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Exponentiate}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 1 = e^4 \implies x = \frac{e^4 + 1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( e^{\square} \) to both sides --- the inverse of \( \ln \) --- to free the argument \( 2x - 1 \), then rearrange for \( x \). Note \( 2x - 1 = e^4 > 0 \) so the log domain is satisfied.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = (e^4 + 1)/2\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Solving Equations 42\quad\small\texttt{(el4-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 42 (el4-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( 2^{2x} - 5 \times 2^x + 4 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let u = 2\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
u^2 - 5u + 4 = 0 \implies (u-1)(u-4) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( 2^{2x} = (2^x)^2 \), the substitution \( u = 2^x \) (with \( u > 0 \) since \( 2^x > 0 \)) converts the equation into a standard quadratic in \( u \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^x = 1 \implies x = 0; \quad 2^x = 4 \implies x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Both roots \( u = 1 \) and \( u = 4 \) are positive so both are valid. Solve each by recognising powers of 2 (or by taking \( \log_2 \)).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = 0 or x = 2
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 43\quad\small\texttt{(el4-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 43 (el4-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( e^{2x} - 4e^x + 3 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let u = e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
(u-1)(u-3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The substitution \( u = e^x \) (\( u > 0 \)) turns the equation into \( u^2 - 4u + 3 = 0 \), which factorises since \( -1 \) and \( -3 \) multiply to \( +3 \) and add to \( -4 \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 1 = 0 \text{ or } x = \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Both roots \( u = 1 \) and \( u = 3 \) are positive so both are valid. Apply \( \ln \) to each to recover \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = 0 or x = ln 3
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 44\quad\small\texttt{(el4-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 44 (el4-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Solve \( \log_2(x + 3) = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Convert}
\[\begin{gathered}
x + 3 = 2^4 = 16 \implies x = 13
\end{gathered}\]
Index form is the inverse of log form: \( \log_a b = c \Leftrightarrow a^c = b \). Subtract 3 then check \( x + 3 = 16 > 0 \) $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 13\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 45\quad\small\texttt{(el4-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 45 (el4-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( \log_3 x + \log_3(x + 6) = 3 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3[x(x+6)] = 3 \implies x^2 + 6x = 27
\end{gathered}\]
The product law \( \log a + \log b = \log(ab) \) merges the two logs into one, then converting to index form gives \( x(x+6) = 3^3 = 27 \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 6x - 27 = 0 \implies (x+9)(x-3) = 0 \implies x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Both \( \log_3 x \) and \( \log_3(x+6) \) require \( x > 0 \), so reject \( x = -9 \) and keep \( x = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 46\quad\small\texttt{(el4-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 46 (el4-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 7^x = 3^{x+2} \). Give answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\ln 7 = (x+2)\ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
With \( x \) in two different exponents, taking \( \ln \) (the inverse of \( e^{\square} \)) brings both exponents down so the equation becomes linear in \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Expand and solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(\ln 7 - \ln 3) = 2\ln 3 \implies x = \frac{2\ln 3}{\ln 7 - \ln 3} = \frac{2.197}{0.847} = 2.59
\end{gathered}\]
Expand, then collect \( x \)-terms on one side and factor. Using \( \ln 7 - \ln 3 = \ln(7/3) \) via the quotient law would also work and keeps the denominator a single log.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 2.59\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 47\quad\small\texttt{(el4-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 47 (el4-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2 \times 3^x = 5 \times 2^x \). Give answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{3^x}{2^x} = \frac{5}{2} \implies \left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^x = 2.5
\end{gathered}\]
Dividing collapses both exponentials into a single base \( 3/2 \), since \( 3^x/2^x = (3/2)^x \). Now there is only one exponential to handle.

\textit{Step 2: Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\ln 2.5}{\ln 1.5} = \frac{0.916}{0.405} = 2.26
\end{gathered}\]
Take \( \ln \) of both sides --- the inverse of the exponential --- and use the power law to bring \( x \) down, then divide.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 2.26\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 48\quad\small\texttt{(el4-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 48 (el4-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( e^x - 6e^{-x} = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Multiply by e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{2x} - e^x - 6 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Multiplying through by \( e^x \) (which is always positive, so safe) clears the \( e^{-x} \) term and reveals the hidden quadratic in \( e^x \).

\textit{Step 2: Let u = e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
(u-3)(u+2) = 0 \implies u = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( u = e^x \) (\( u > 0 \)) and factorise. Reject \( u = -2 \) because \( e^x > 0 \) for all real \( x \) --- this validity check is essential.

\textit{Step 3: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( \ln \) --- the inverse of \( e^{\square} \) --- to \( e^x = 3 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = ln 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 49\quad\small\texttt{(el4-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 49 (el4-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \log_5(2x - 1) - \log_5(x + 2) = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_5\frac{2x-1}{x+2} = 1 \implies \frac{2x-1}{x+2} = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Use the quotient law to merge into one log, then convert to index form (\( 5^1 = 5 \)) to remove the logarithm.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 1 = 5(x+2) \implies 2x - 1 = 5x + 10 \implies -3x = 11 \implies x = -\frac{11}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply out and rearrange. Always check the log domain: at \( x = -\tfrac{11}{3} \), \( 2x - 1 = -\tfrac{25}{3} < 0 \), so the original \( \log_5(2x-1) \) is undefined and the candidate must be rejected.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No solution
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 50\quad\small\texttt{(el4-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 50 (el4-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{A population grows according to \( P = 500e^{0.03t} \). Find \( t \) when \( P = 1000 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{0.03t} = 2 \implies 0.03t = \ln 2 \implies t = \frac{\ln 2}{0.03} = 23.1
\end{gathered}\]
Setting \( P = 1000 \) gives \( 500e^{0.03t} = 1000 \), so \( e^{0.03t} = 2 \). Apply \( \ln \) --- the inverse of \( e^{\square} \) --- to bring the exponent down, then divide by 0.03.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad t = 23.1 years
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 51\quad\small\texttt{(el4-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 51 (el4-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 4^x = 2^{x+3} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write 4 as 2\textasciicircum 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^{2x} = 2^{x+3} \implies 2x = x + 3 \implies x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Rewriting \( 4 = 2^2 \) gives matching bases on both sides. With equal positive bases, the exponents must be equal --- no logs needed.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 52\quad\small\texttt{(el4-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 52 (el4-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 9^x = 3^{x+4} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Write 9 as 3\textasciicircum 2}
\[\begin{gathered}
3^{2x} = 3^{x+4} \implies 2x = x + 4 \implies x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Writing \( 9 = 3^2 \) gives the same base on both sides, so the exponents must be equal. This avoids the need for logs entirely.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 53\quad\small\texttt{(el4-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 53 (el4-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( 5e^{2x} - 3e^x = 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let u = e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
5u^2 - 3u - 2 = 0 \implies (5u + 2)(u - 1) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( e^{2x} = (e^x)^2 \), the substitution \( u = e^x \) (\( u > 0 \)) converts the equation into a quadratic. Check the factorisation: \( 5u^2 - 5u + 2u - 2 = 5u^2 - 3u - 2 \) $\checkmark$.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
u = 1 \implies x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Reject \( u = -\tfrac{2}{5} \) because \( e^x > 0 \) always. The valid root \( u = 1 \) gives \( e^x = 1 \Rightarrow x = 0 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 0\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 54\quad\small\texttt{(el4-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 54 (el4-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \ln x + \ln(x - 2) = \ln 15 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
x(x-2) = 15 \implies x^2 - 2x - 15 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the product law on the left, then use \( \ln A = \ln B \Rightarrow A = B \) (because \( \ln \) is one-to-one) to remove the logs.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x-5)(x+3) = 0 \implies x = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Both \( \ln x \) and \( \ln(x-2) \) require \( x > 2 \), so reject \( x = -3 \) and keep \( x = 5 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 5\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 55\quad\small\texttt{(el4-055)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 55 (el4-055)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{The half-life of a substance is 10 hours. If \( m = m_0 e^{-kt} \), find \( k \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{At t = 10, m = m\_0/2}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{1}{2} = e^{-10k} \implies -10k = \ln \frac{1}{2} \implies k = \frac{\ln 2}{10}
\end{gathered}\]
After one half-life the mass is halved, so \( m_0/2 = m_0 e^{-10k} \) gives \( e^{-10k} = 1/2 \). Apply \( \ln \) (the inverse of \( e^{\square} \)) and use \( \ln(1/2) = -\ln 2 \) so the negatives cancel.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle k = (ln 2)/10 = 0.0693\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 56\quad\small\texttt{(el4-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 56 (el4-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2\log_3 x = \log_3(5x + 6) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Power rule on LHS}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log_3 x^2 = \log_3(5x+6) \implies x^2 = 5x + 6
\end{gathered}\]
Use the power law \( n\log a = \log a^n \) to merge the LHS into a single log, then equate arguments because \( \log_3 \) is one-to-one.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 - 5x - 6 = 0 \implies (x-6)(x+1) = 0 \implies x = 6
\end{gathered}\]
The original \( \log_3 x \) requires \( x > 0 \), so reject \( x = -1 \) and keep \( x = 6 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 6\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 57\quad\small\texttt{(el4-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 57 (el4-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 3^{x+1} = 4^{x-1} \). Give answer to 3 s.f.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+1)\ln 3 = (x-1)\ln 4
\end{gathered}\]
With \( x \) in two different exponents, taking \( \ln \) is the inverse step that brings both exponents down --- turning the equation into a linear one in \( x \).

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x\ln 3 + \ln 3 = x\ln 4 - \ln 4 \implies x(\ln 3 - \ln 4) = -\ln 4 - \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Expand the brackets, then collect \( x \)-terms on one side and constants on the other before factoring.

\textit{Step 3: Calculate}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{-(\ln 4 + \ln 3)}{\ln 3 - \ln 4} = \frac{\ln 12}{\ln(4/3)} = 8.64
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the product law \( \ln 4 + \ln 3 = \ln 12 \) and quotient law \( \ln 4 - \ln 3 = \ln(4/3) \) --- combining logs makes the arithmetic simpler. The two minus signs cancel.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 8.64\)
\end{answerbox}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Multiply by e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{2x} - 3e^x + 2 = 0 \implies (e^x - 1)(e^x - 2) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Multiplying by \( e^x \) (always positive, so safe) eliminates the \( e^{-x} \) term and reveals a quadratic in \( e^x \), which factorises easily.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 0 \text{ or } x = \ln 2
\end{gathered}\]
Both \( e^x = 1 \) and \( e^x = 2 \) are positive so both are valid. Apply \( \ln \) to each: \( \ln 1 = 0 \) and \( \ln 2 \) is the exact form of the other root.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = 0 or x = ln 2
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 59\quad\small\texttt{(el4-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 59 (el4-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( \log(x + 5) + \log(x - 2) = \log 18 \). (Base 10)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+5)(x-2) = 18 \implies x^2 + 3x - 28 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the product law to merge the logs into one, then equate arguments (\( \log \) is one-to-one). Expand and rearrange into standard quadratic form.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
(x+7)(x-4) = 0 \implies x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Both \( \log(x+5) \) and \( \log(x-2) \) require \( x > 2 \), so reject \( x = -7 \) and keep \( x = 4 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 60\quad\small\texttt{(el4-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 60 (el4-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 2^x + 2^{x+2} = 40 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factor}
\[\begin{gathered}
2^x(1 + 2^2) = 40 \implies 5 \times 2^x = 40 \implies 2^x = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Use the index law \( 2^{x+2} = 2^x \cdot 2^2 \) so we can factor out the common \( 2^x \). This is much cleaner than taking logs of a sum.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Recognise \( 8 = 2^3 \), so by matching bases \( x = 3 \). No logs required.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 3\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 61\quad\small\texttt{(el4-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 61 (el4-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( 3^{x+1} - 3^x = 18 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Factor}
\[\begin{gathered}
3^x(3 - 1) = 18 \implies 2 \times 3^x = 18 \implies 3^x = 9
\end{gathered}\]
Write \( 3^{x+1} = 3 \cdot 3^x \) to expose the common factor \( 3^x \). This avoids needing logs.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Recognise \( 9 = 3^2 \), so matching bases gives \( x = 2 \) directly.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 2\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 62\quad\small\texttt{(el4-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 62 (el4-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Solve \( \ln(x^2) = 6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Exponentiate to remove the log}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = e^6
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( e^{\square} \) --- the inverse of \( \ln \) --- to both sides. Note that \( \ln(x^2) \) is defined whenever \( x \neq 0 \), so both signs of \( x \) are possible.

\textit{Step 2: Take the square root, keeping both signs}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \pm\sqrt{e^6} = \pm e^3
\end{gathered}\]
Pitfall: the power law \( \ln(x^2) = 2\ln x \) only holds for \( x > 0 \), so we must not use it here without care. Working directly from \( x^2 = e^6 \) gives both \( x = e^3 \) and \( x = -e^3 \), and both satisfy the original equation since \( (-e^3)^2 = e^6 \) too.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = e\textasciicircum 3 or x = -e\textasciicircum 3
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Solving Equations 63\quad\small\texttt{(el4-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 63 (el4-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{An investment of \( \pounds 2000 \) grows to \( \pounds 3000 \) at 4\% compound interest. Find the number of years.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Set up}
\[\begin{gathered}
2000 \times 1.04^n = 3000 \implies 1.04^n = 1.5
\end{gathered}\]
The compound interest model is \( P \times (1 + r)^n \). Set this equal to 3000 and divide by the initial 2000 to isolate the exponential factor.

\textit{Step 2: Take logs}
\[\begin{gathered}
n = \frac{\ln 1.5}{\ln 1.04} = \frac{0.405}{0.0392} = 10.3
\end{gathered}\]
Taking \( \ln \) of both sides --- the inverse of an exponential --- brings the exponent \( n \) down via the power law. Since the answer 10.3 isn't a whole year, we round up to 11 because the investment must have completed enough years to exceed \pounds 3000.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad 11 years (round up)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 64\quad\small\texttt{(el4-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 64 (el4-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve simultaneously: \( \log_2 x + \log_2 y = 5 \) and \( \log_2 x - \log_2 y = 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Add equations}
\[\begin{gathered}
2\log_2 x = 6 \implies \log_2 x = 3 \implies x = 8
\end{gathered}\]
Treat \( \log_2 x \) and \( \log_2 y \) as two unknowns. Adding the equations eliminates \( \log_2 y \); converting to index form (\( 2^3 = 8 \)) is the inverse of the log and gives \( x \) directly.

\textit{Step 2: Subtract}
\[\begin{gathered}
2\log_2 y = 4 \implies y = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Subtracting the second equation from the first eliminates \( \log_2 x \), giving \( \log_2 y = 2 \), so \( y = 2^2 = 4 \). Both \( x, y > 0 \), so the original logs are well-defined $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 8, y = 4\)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 65\quad\small\texttt{(el4-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 65 (el4-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( e^{2x} + 3e^x - 10 = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Let u = e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
(u+5)(u-2) = 0 \implies u = 2
\end{gathered}\]
The substitution \( u = e^x \) (\( u > 0 \)) gives the quadratic \( u^2 + 3u - 10 = 0 \). Reject \( u = -5 \) because \( e^x \) cannot be negative.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 2
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( \ln \) --- the inverse of \( e^{\square} \) --- to the valid root \( e^x = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = ln 2\)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 66\quad\small\texttt{(el4-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 66 (el4-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( (\log x)^2 = \log x^4 \). (Base 10)}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Simplify RHS}
\[\begin{gathered}
(\log x)^2 = 4\log x
\end{gathered}\]
Use the power law \( \log x^4 = 4\log x \) on the right. Note: be careful --- \( (\log x)^2 \) on the left is the log squared, not the log of \( x^2 \), so do not collapse it further.

\textit{Step 2: Rearrange}
\[\begin{gathered}
(\log x)^2 - 4\log x = 0 \implies \log x(\log x - 4) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Treat \( \log x \) as a single quantity and factor it out --- like solving \( u^2 - 4u = u(u-4) = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
\log x = 0: x = 1; \quad \log x = 4: x = 10000
\end{gathered}\]
Convert each case to index form: \( \log x = 0 \Rightarrow x = 10^0 = 1 \), and \( \log x = 4 \Rightarrow x = 10^4 = 10000 \). Both are positive, so both are valid $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = 1 or x = 10000
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 67\quad\small\texttt{(el4-067)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 67 (el4-067)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Solve \( 2\ln(x + 1) - \ln x = \ln 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Combine}
\[\begin{gathered}
\ln\frac{(x+1)^2}{x} = \ln 4 \implies \frac{(x+1)^2}{x} = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Use the power law \( 2\ln(x+1) = \ln(x+1)^2 \) and the quotient law to merge into a single logarithm. Since \( \ln \) is one-to-one, equate the arguments.

\textit{Step 2: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 + 2x + 1 = 4x \implies x^2 - 2x + 1 = 0 \implies (x-1)^2 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Expand, rearrange, and recognise a perfect square. The repeated root \( x = 1 \) satisfies \( x > 0 \), so the log arguments are valid $\checkmark$.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = 1\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 68\quad\small\texttt{(el4-068)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 68 (el4-068)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A cooling object has temperature \( T = 25 + 60e^{-0.04t} \). Find when \( T = 40 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Substitute}
\[\begin{gathered}
15 = 60e^{-0.04t} \implies e^{-0.04t} = 0.25
\end{gathered}\]
Set \( T = 40 \), then subtract 25 (the ambient temperature) and divide by 60 to isolate the exponential before applying \( \ln \).

\textit{Step 2: Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
-0.04t = \ln 0.25 = -1.386 \implies t = 34.7 \text{ min}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \ln \) is the inverse of \( e^{\square} \), so the exponent comes down. The two negatives cancel when dividing by \( -0.04 \), giving a sensibly positive time.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad t = 34.7 minutes
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Solving Equations 69\quad\small\texttt{(el4-069)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 69 (el4-069)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the exact solution of \( 3^x = 2 \times 5^x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left(\frac{3}{5}\right)^x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Dividing \( 3^x / 5^x = (3/5)^x \) collapses the two exponentials into one, isolating a single exponential with base \( 3/5 \).

\textit{Step 2: Take ln}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \frac{\ln 2}{\ln(3/5)} = \frac{\ln 2}{\ln 3 - \ln 5}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( \ln \) (the inverse of an exponential) to bring \( x \) down via the power law. The quotient law \( \ln(3/5) = \ln 3 - \ln 5 \) gives the alternative form. Note: \( \ln(3/5) < 0 \), so \( x < 0 \) --- consistent with needing to raise a number less than 1 to a negative power to get a result greater than 1.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle x = ln 2 / (ln 3 - ln 5)\)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Solving Equations 70\quad\small\texttt{(el4-070)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Solving Equations 70 (el4-070)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Solve \( e^{2x} - 5e^x = -6 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rearrange}
\[\begin{gathered}
e^{2x} - 5e^x + 6 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Move all terms to one side to get standard quadratic-in-\( e^x \) form before substituting.

\textit{Step 2: Let u = e\textasciicircum x}
\[\begin{gathered}
(u-2)(u-3) = 0 \implies u = 2 \text{ or } u = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Since \( e^{2x} = (e^x)^2 \), the substitution \( u = e^x \) (\( u > 0 \)) gives \( u^2 - 5u + 6 = 0 \). Both roots are positive, so both are valid.

\textit{Step 3: Solve}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = \ln 2 \text{ or } x = \ln 3
\end{gathered}\]
Apply \( \ln \) --- the inverse of \( e^{\square} \) --- to each case to recover \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad x = ln 2 or x = ln 3
\end{answerbox}
